<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01N2K7KXqzM2MaEKYfsesnqo`</summary>

```text
1	
2	\section{Prompt Templates}
3	\label{app:prompts}
4	
5	SICA uses domain-specific prompt templates for constraint extraction: one for mathematical reasoning (GSM8K, AIME) and one for logical reasoning (ProofWriter, FOLIO). Both share the same system prompt. The LLM is called with temperature $T{=}0.1$, max tokens $1024$, and structured JSON output mode. Up to 6 traces are processed in parallel via thread pooling, with 2 retries on failure (exponential backoff, base 0.5s).
6	
7	\paragraph{System Prompt.}
8	
9	\begin{lstlisting}
10	You are a constraint extraction tool. Output ONLY
11	valid JSON. Do NOT solve problems, reason about
12	correctness, or think step-by-step. Extract
13	constraints from the given trace exactly as stated.
14	\end{lstlisting}
15	
16	\paragraph{Mathematical Constraint Extraction (EXTRACTION\_PROMPT).}
17	
18	Used for GSM8K and AIME datasets. The placeholder \texttt{\{trace\}} is replaced with the reasoning trace at runtime.
19	
20	\begin{lstlisting}
21	Extract mathematical constraints from this reasoning
22	trace as a JSON object.
23	
24	Rules:
25	- type: "equation", "inequality", or "predicate"
26	- expression: human-readable, e.g. "x + y == 14"
27	- z3_formula: a single comparison expression in
28	  Python Z3 syntax
29	- source_step: step number where the constraint
30	  appears
31	- Use variable names from the trace
32	
33	z3_formula MUST follow these rules:
34	- Allowed operators: ==, !=, <, >, <=, >=, +, -, *,
35	  /, **, %
36	- Use individual named variables: a1, a2, a3...
37	  NOT arrays a[1] or sets A
38	- Expand all sums explicitly:
39	  "a1 + a2 + a3 == 55" NOT "sum([a1,a2,a3])"
40	- NO Python builtins: sum(), len(), max(), min(),
41	  list(), set(), range(), int()
42	- Each z3_formula must be exactly ONE comparison
43	  (==, !=, <, >, <=, >=)
44	- For multiple values, write separate constraints
45	  for each
46	
47	Output ONLY the JSON below, nothing else:
48	{"constraints": [{"type": "equation",
49	  "expression": "x + y == 14",
50	  "z3_formula": "x + y == 14",
51	  "source_step": 1}],
52	 "answer": "final_answer",
53	 "variables": ["x", "y"]}
54	
55	Trace:
56	{trace}
57	\end{lstlisting}
58	
59	\paragraph{Logical Constraint Extraction (LOGIC\_EXTRACTION\_PROMPT).}
60	
61	Used for ProofWriter and FOLIO datasets.
62	
63	\begin{lstlisting}
64	IMPORTANT: You are an EXTRACTION tool, NOT a problem
65	solver.
66	- Do NOT re-solve or re-reason about the problem.
67	- Do NOT verify whether the trace's reasoning is
68	  correct.
69	- ONLY extract the logical constraints that are
70	  explicitly stated or used in the trace below.
71	- Output ONLY the JSON object, nothing else.
72	
73	Extract logical constraints from this reasoning trace
74	about a logic problem.
75	
76	The problem has facts (base assertions), rules
77	(grounded implications), and derived conclusions.
78	Convert each into a Z3 boolean formula.
79	
80	Variable naming:
81	- Unary property: property_entity
82	  (e.g., kind_anne, red_charlie, round_fiona)
83	- Binary relation: relation_subject_object
84	  (e.g., eats_bear_squirrel, likes_lion_cat)
85	- Use lowercase with underscores only
86	
87	Constraint types:
88	- "fact": a given assertion,
89	  e.g., kind_anne == True
90	- "rule": a grounded implication over specific
91	  entities, e.g., Implies(kind_anne, nice_anne)
92	- "derived": an inferred conclusion from facts +
93	  rules, e.g., nice_anne == True
94	
95	Z3 operators:
96	- Implies(a, b) for if-then rules
97	- And(a, b, ...) for conjunctive conditions
98	- Or(a, b, ...) for disjunctive conditions
99	- Not(a) for negation
100	- == True / == False for boolean assertions
101	
102	Output ONLY the JSON below, nothing else:
103	{"constraints": [{"type": "fact",
104	  "expression": "kind(anne)",
105	  "z3_formula": "kind_anne == True",
106	  "source_step": 1}],
107	 "answer": "True or False or Unknown",
108	 "variables": ["kind_anne"]}
109	
110	Trace:
111	{trace}
112	\end{lstlisting}
113	
114	\paragraph{Output Schema.}
115	
116	Both prompts produce a JSON object with the following structure:
117	
118	\begin{lstlisting}
119	{
120	  "constraints": [
121	    {
122	      "type": "&lt;equation|inequality|predicate>
123	               or &lt;fact|rule|derived>",
124	      "expression": "&lt;human-readable description>",
125	      "z3_formula": "&lt;Z3-compatible expression>",
126	      "source_step": &lt;integer>
127	    }
128	  ],
129	  "answer": "&lt;final answer string>",
130	  "variables": ["&lt;var1>", "&lt;var2>", "..."]
131	}
132	\end{lstlisting}
133	
134	The pipeline additionally injects an \texttt{answer\_assertion} constraint per trace (not produced by the LLM):
135	
136	\begin{lstlisting}
137	{"type": "answer_assertion",
138	 "expression": "answer is &lt;ans>",
139	 "z3_formula": "__sica_answer_&lt;ans> == True",
140	 "source_step": -1}
141	\end{lstlisting}
142	
143	\paragraph{Prompt Selection Logic.}
144	
145	Domain assignment is determined by dataset: FOLIO and ProofWriter use \textsc{Logic\_Extraction\_Prompt}; GSM8K, AIME, StrategyQA, and ARC-Challenge use \textsc{Extraction\_Prompt}. StrategyQA and ARC-Challenge are assigned to the mathematical prompt because their reasoning structure does not involve formal logic predicates or implications, despite StrategyQA producing boolean answers.
146	
147	
148	\section{Z3 MaxSAT Evaluation Pseudocode}
149	\label{app:z3-pseudocode}
150	
151	Algorithm~\ref{alg:maxsat} describes the Z3 MaxSAT solving procedure. Algorithm~\ref{alg:scorer} describes the source-trace attribution scoring.
152	
153	Domain constraint variables (e.g., \texttt{x + y == 14}) and answer assertion variables (e.g., \texttt{\_\_sica\_answer\_42 == True}) are logically independent in the Z3 formulation. Discriminative signal arises solely from conflicts among domain constraints: when traces supporting different answers produce mutually contradictory constraints, the solver excludes the lower-weight subset, and the scorer penalizes the corresponding answers.
154	
155	\begin{algorithm}[h]
156	\caption{Z3 Weighted Partial MAX-SAT}
157	\label{alg:maxsat}
158	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
159	\Require Deduplicated constraint set $\mathcal{C} = \{(c_m, w_m, S_m)\}_{m=1}^M$ where $c_m$ is a Z3 formula, $w_m$ is weight, $S_m$ is the set of source trace indices
160	\Ensure Partition $(\mathcal{C}^*, \mathcal{C}^{\setminus})$ where $\mathcal{C}^*$ is the max-weight satisfiable subset
161	\State $\textit{opt} \gets \textsc{Z3.Optimize()}$
162	\For{$m = 1, \ldots, M$}
163	    \State $b_m \gets$ fresh Boolean indicator
164	    \State $\textit{opt}.\textsc{Add}(b_m \Rightarrow c_m)$ \Comment{soft clause guarded by indicator}
165	    \State $\textit{opt}.\textsc{AddSoft}(b_m,\; \text{weight}=w_m)$
166	\EndFor
167	\State $\textit{result} \gets \textit{opt}.\textsc{Check}()$
168	\State $\textit{model} \gets \textit{opt}.\textsc{Model}()$
169	\State $\mathcal{C}^* \gets \{(c_m, w_m, S_m) : \textit{model}(b_m) = \top\}$
170	\State $\mathcal{C}^{\setminus} \gets \mathcal{C} \setminus \mathcal{C}^*$
171	\State \Return $(\mathcal{C}^*, \mathcal{C}^{\setminus})$
172	\end{algorithmic}
173	\end{algorithm}
174	
175	\begin{algorithm}[h]
176	\caption{Source-Trace Attribution Scoring}
177	\label{alg:scorer}
178	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
179	\Require Satisfied set $\mathcal{C}^*$, excluded set $\mathcal{C}^{\setminus}$, candidate answers $\mathcal{A}$, trace-answer mapping $\tau: \text{trace\_idx} \to a$, penalty weight $\alpha$
180	\Ensure Score $s(a)$ for each $a \in \mathcal{A}$
181	\For{each $a \in \mathcal{A}$}
182	    \State $T_a \gets \{i : \tau(i) = a\}$ \Comment{trace indices producing answer $a$}
183	    \State $\textit{pos}(a) \gets \sum_{(c,w,S) \in \mathcal{C}^*} w \cdot \mathbf{1}[S \cap T_a \neq \emptyset]$
184	    \State $\textit{neg}(a) \gets \sum_{(c,w,S) \in \mathcal{C}^{\setminus}} w \cdot \mathbf{1}[S \cap T_a \neq \emptyset]$
185	    \State $s(a) \gets \textit{pos}(a) - \alpha \cdot \textit{neg}(a)$
186	\EndFor
187	\State \Return $a^* = \arg\max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} s(a)$
188	\end{algorithmic}
189	\end{algorithm}
190	
191

194

205	Qwen2.5-14B & ProofWriter & 39.2 & 0.89 & 0.022 & $+$0.33 \\
206	Qwen2.5-14B & StrategyQA$^\dagger$ & 10.0 & 0.70 & --- & $+$3.33 \\

Extraction rate: fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable FOL constraint. Contradiction rate: fraction of extracted constraint sets with internal contradictions. $\Delta$: SICA$-$SC gap (pp). $\dagger$: $n \leq 60$. Dashes indicate conditions where extraction diagnostics were not computed (StrategyQA uses boolean answers without FOL extraction; QwQ-32B Combined had near-zero $C_0$, making extraction analysis uninformative).}

220	
221	
222	\section{Statistical Power Analysis}
223	\label{app:power}
224	
225	We compute the minimum detectable effect (MDE) for each condition at $\alpha = 0.05$ and 80\% power using exact binomial power calculations over McNemar discordant pairs. Table~\ref{tab:power} reports three grouped conditions with $n \geq 200$ (Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO and ProofWriter are pooled into a single $n{=}804$ analysis, corresponding to four Table~\ref{tab:main_results} rows), which constitute our confirmatory analyses. The remaining seven conditions ($n \leq 60$) yield infinite MDE due to insufficient discordant pairs and serve only as regime characterization.
226	
227	\begin{table}[h]
228	\centering
229	\small
230	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}
231	\toprule
232	\textbf{Condition} & $n$ & \textbf{MDE} & \textbf{Obs.\ $\Delta$} & \textbf{Obs.\ Pow.} \\
233	\midrule
234	Qwen-14B FOLIO+PW   & 804 & 1.86pp & $+$0.25pp & 5.7\%  \\
235	LLaMA-8B StrategyQA  & 200 & 4.64pp & $+$2.00pp & 20.6\% \\
236	LLaMA-8B FOLIO       & 204 & 4.84pp & $-$2.45pp & 18.6\% \\
237	\bottomrule
238	\end{tabular}
239	\caption{Power analysis for confirmatory conditions ($n \geq 200$). MDE = minimum detectable effect at 80\% power. All observed effects fall below their respective MDEs, and observed power remains below 21\% in every case.}
240	\label{tab:power}
241	\end{table}
242	
243	The largest study (Qwen-14B, $n{=}804$, 30 discordant pairs) can detect effects as small as 1.86pp, yet the observed $\Delta$ of $+$0.25pp is an order of magnitude smaller. The two LLaMA-8B conditions require effects of 4.64pp and 4.84pp to reach significance; neither observed $\Delta$ approaches these thresholds. All three conditions are consistent with the null hypothesis that SICA provides no accuracy gain over self-consistency.
244	
245	
246	\section{Bootstrap Confidence Intervals}
247	\label{app:bootstrap}
248	
249	We compute 95\% confidence intervals for $\Delta(\text{SICA} - \text{SC})$ via 10{,}000 bootstrap resamples (percentile method, seed 42). Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} reports the results for the three confirmatory conditions.
250	
251	\begin{table}[h]
252	\centering
253	\small
254	\begin{tabular}{lrrl}
255	\toprule
256	\textbf{Condition} & $n$ & \textbf{Obs.\ $\Delta$} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
257	\midrule
258	Qwen-14B FOLIO+PW   & 804 & $+$0.25pp & [$-$1.12, $+$1.62] \\
259	LLaMA-8B StrategyQA  & 200 & $+$2.00pp & [$-$1.50, $+$5.50] \\
260	LLaMA-8B FOLIO       & 204 & $-$2.45pp & [$-$5.88, $+$0.98] \\
261	\bottomrule
262	\end{tabular}
263	\caption{Bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals for $\Delta(\text{SICA} - \text{SC})$ in percentage points. All intervals include zero, consistent with no reliable accuracy difference.}
264	\label{tab:bootstrap}
265	\end{table}
266	
267	All three intervals contain zero. The narrowest interval (Qwen-14B, [$-$1.12, $+$1.62]) rules out practically meaningful gains ($>$2pp) even in the most favorable case. The widest interval (LLaMA-8B FOLIO, [$-$5.88, $+$0.98]) skews negative, indicating that SICA may slightly hurt accuracy in this condition. These results corroborate the McNemar tests and power analysis: no condition provides evidence that symbolic constraint aggregation improves over majority voting.
268	
269	
270	\section{ProofWriter Oracle Per-Subtype Analysis}
271	\label{app:pw-oracle-subtype}
272	
273	ProofWriter problems encode deductive rules as Domain-Specific Language (DSL) logic programs. Each problem uses one of four predicate subtypes: attribute predicates with or without negation (AttNeg, AttNoneg), and relation predicates with or without negation (RelNeg, RelNoneg). Relation predicates involve binary relations between entities (e.g., \texttt{eats(bear, squirrel)}), while attribute predicates assign unary properties (e.g., \texttt{kind(anne)}).
274	
275	The oracle pipeline parses these DSL annotations deterministically into Z3 formulas without any LLM involvement. Of the 600 ProofWriter problems, 594 (99.0\%) produce valid Z3 parses; the remaining 6 fail due to unsupported DSL constructs. Across all 7{,}128 trace-problem pairs submitted to Z3, the solver returns a result in every case (0\% solver errors). Z3 evaluations agree with ground-truth labels on 577 of 594 parseable problems (97.14\%), confirming that the deterministic DSL-to-Z3 translation faithfully preserves problem semantics.
276	
277	Table~\ref{tab:pw_oracle_subtype} reports the per-subtype breakdown. Relation-predicate subtypes show substantially larger gains than attribute-predicate subtypes: RelNeg improves by $+17.86$~pp and RelNoneg by $+13.01$~pp, compared to $+9.09$~pp for AttNoneg and $+6.87$~pp for AttNeg. Relation predicates involve longer reasoning chains with more intermediate entities, producing greater answer diversity across traces ($C_0$) and more opportunities for oracle constraints to correct errors.
278	
279	\begin{table}[h]
280	\centering
281	\caption{ProofWriter oracle experiment per-subtype breakdown. Oracle-SICA uses gold DSL annotations parsed deterministically to Z3 for consistency checking. Relation-predicate subtypes (RelNeg, RelNoneg) show larger gains than attribute-predicate subtypes, suggesting longer reasoning chains benefit more from oracle constraints. Bold indicates best accuracy per row.}
282	\label{tab:pw_oracle_subtype}
283	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
284	\toprule
285	Subtype & $n$ & SC Acc (\%) & Oracle Acc (\%) & $\Delta$ (pp) \\
286	\midrule
287	AttNoneg & 154 & 87.01 & \textbf{96.10} & +9.09 \\
288	AttNeg & 160 & 85.00 & \textbf{91.88} & +6.87 \\
289	RelNoneg & 146 & 66.44 & \textbf{79.45} & +13.01 \\
290	RelNeg & 140 & 60.00 & \textbf{77.86} & +17.86 \\
291	\midrule
292	Overall & 600 & 75.17 & \textbf{86.67} & +11.50 \\
293	\bottomrule
294	\end{tabular}
295	\end{table}
296	
```

The narrowest interval (Qwen-14B, [$-$1.12, $+$1.62]) rules out practically meaningful gains ($>$2pp) even in the most favorable case. The widest interval (LLaMA-8B FOLIO, [$-$5.88, $+$0.98]) skews negative, indicating that SICA may slightly hurt accuracy in this condition. These results corroborate the McNemar tests and power analysis: no condition provides evidence that symbolic constraint aggregation improves over majority voting.\n\n\n\\section{ProofWriter Oracle Per-Subtype Analysis}\n\\label{app:pw-oracle-subtype}\n\nProofWriter problems encode deductive rules as Domain-Specific Language (DSL) logic programs. Each problem uses one of four predicate subtypes: attribute predicates with or without negation (AttNeg, AttNoneg), and relation predicates with or without negation (RelNeg, RelNoneg). Relation predicates involve binary relations between entities (e.g., \\texttt{eats(bear, squirrel)}), while attribute predicates assign unary properties (e.g., \\texttt{kind(anne)}).\n\nThe oracle pipeline parses these DSL annotations deterministically into Z3 formulas without any LLM involvement. Of the 600 ProofWriter problems, 594 (99.0\\%) produce valid Z3 parses; the remaining 6 fail due to unsupported DSL constructs. Across all 7{,}128 trace-problem pairs submitted to Z3, the solver returns a result in every case (0\\% solver errors). Z3 evaluations agree with ground-truth labels on 577 of 594 parseable problems (97.14\\%), confirming that the deterministic DSL-to-Z3 translation faithfully preserves problem semantics.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:pw_oracle_subtype} reports the per-subtype breakdown. Relation-predicate subtypes show substantially larger gains than attribute-predicate subtypes: RelNeg improves by $+17.86$~pp and RelNoneg by $+13.01$~pp, compared to $+9.09$~pp for AttNoneg and $+6.87$~pp for AttNeg. Relation predicates involve longer reasoning chains with more intermediate entities, producing greater answer diversity across traces ($C_0$) and more opportunities for oracle constraints to correct errors.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{ProofWriter oracle experiment per-subtype breakdown. Oracle-SICA uses gold DSL annotations parsed deterministically to Z3 for consistency checking. Relation-predicate subtypes (RelNeg, RelNoneg) show larger gains than attribute-predicate subtypes, suggesting longer reasoning chains benefit more from oracle constraints. Bold indicates best accuracy per row.}\n\\label{tab:pw_oracle_subtype}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nSubtype & $n$ & SC Acc (\\%) & Oracle Acc (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nAttNoneg & 154 & 87.01 & \\textbf{96.10} & +9.09 \\\\\nAttNeg & 160 & 85.00 & \\textbf{91.88} & +6.87 \\\\\nRelNoneg & 146 & 66.44 & \\textbf{79.45} & +13.01 \\\\\nRelNeg & 140 & 60.00 & \\textbf{77.86} & +17.86 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOverall & 600 & 75.17 & \\textbf{86.67} & +11.50 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",
```json
      "numLines": 296,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 296
    }
```

_source line 801 · 2026-05-18T06:15:12.329Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017J1vdW7qsucW5cMnKWWLCS`</summary>

```text
18	
19	\paragraph{Debate and Multi-Agent Aggregation.}
20	Multi-agent debate refines answers through iterative natural language discussion among LLM instances \citep{liang2023encouraging,du2023improving}. In debate, models exchange arguments in plain text and update their positions based on peer responses. SICA takes a structurally different approach: instead of natural language refinement, it extracts formal constraints and resolves disagreements through satisfiability optimization. Both families attempt to move beyond simple voting, but debate preserves natural language flexibility while SICA commits to formal structure. \citet{huang2024selfcorrect} show that LLMs struggle to self-correct reasoning without external feedback, and \citet{mirzadeh2024gsmsymbolic} demonstrate that LLM mathematical reasoning is fragile under symbolic perturbations. \citet{stechly2025selfverification} provide the most direct parallel: they show that LLM self-verification on reasoning and planning tasks degrades performance, while sound external verification yields significant gains. Their finding that verification is not inherently easier than generation for these models is structurally isomorphic to our capability trap, but operates in natural language self-critique rather than FOL+SAT. Our contribution extends this line by demonstrating the same limitation in a formal symbolic setting: even when self-extracted constraints are syntactically valid FOL and processed by a sound solver, they carry no discriminative signal beyond vote counting. \citet{valmeekam2023planning} and \citet{kambhampati2024position} further establish that LLMs lack autonomous planning ability and require external modules in ``LLM-Modulo'' frameworks to achieve reliable performance, reinforcing the pattern that self-reliance is the bottleneck. Across all six lines of work reviewed here, the consistent pattern is that improving on majority voting requires information beyond what the reasoning model can extract from its own outputs, whether from trained verifiers, external solvers, human annotations, or cross-model interaction.
```

</details>

_source line 802 · 2026-05-18T06:15:13.781Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01XLtTUftULc4gtp9Exj5LPd`</summary>

```text
71	\text{score}(a) = \sum_{c \in \mathcal{C}^*} w_c \cdot \sigma(c, a) \;-\; \alpha \sum_{c \in \mathcal{C}^{\setminus}} w_c \cdot \sigma(c, a),
72	\end{equation}
73	where $\sigma(c, a) \in \{0, 1\}$ is a source-trace indicator: $\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if $c$ was extracted from any trace that produced answer $a$, and 0 otherwise. The penalty weight $\alpha = 0.5$ in all experiments. The first term rewards answers whose source constraints are satisfied; the second penalizes answers whose source constraints were excluded by MAX-SAT. Discriminative signal arises when constraints from different traces conflict: Z3 excludes low-weight constraints from $\mathcal{C}^*$, and the scorer attributes these exclusions to the corresponding source answers via $\sigma$ (see Algorithm~\ref{alg:scorer} in Appendix~\ref{app:z3-pseudocode}). To ensure each candidate answer participates in scoring, the pipeline injects an \emph{answer assertion} constraint $\texttt{answer\_a} \mathrel{{=}{=}} \texttt{True}$ for each trace, with weight 1. This guarantees that every candidate receives a nonzero base score proportional to its vote count; SICA is therefore a strict generalization of SC. The selected answer is $a^* = \arg\max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \text{score}(a)$.
74	
75	\paragraph{Degeneration under non-discriminative constraints.}
```

_source line 804 · 2026-05-18T06:15:13.133Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JjgGyJB6YmDJGDNDH3mCLo`</summary>

```text
10	The oracle ceilings ($+8.82$\,pp on FOLIO, $+11.50$\,pp on ProofWriter) quantify the potential locked behind the capability trap, with larger gains on harder relational reasoning subtypes (RelNeg $+17.86$\,pp). The oracle gap suggests that constraint quality, not the extraction prompt format, is the primary bottleneck, though alternative extraction strategies (e.g., few-shot examples, iterative refinement, structured decomposition) were not tested in this study. Three concrete paths can unlock the potential. First, trained FOL extractors supervised on datasets with gold annotations (such as FOLIO) can learn extraction patterns that exceed same-model capabilities; FoVer \citep{wang2025fover} demonstrates this approach with a dedicated verifier. Second, external provers that verify constraints independently of the reasoning model eliminate the circular dependency between generation and verification. Third, cross-model extraction, where a separate model extracts constraints from another model's traces, breaks the self-reference loop that produces non-discriminative constraints. Each path targets the extraction bottleneck directly; since MAX-SAT and count-only produce identical predictions on 803/804 instances, improving the aggregation mechanism offers no path forward.
11	
12	For practitioners considering constraint-based voting, $C_0$ provides a zero-cost screening criterion computable from the trace sampling stage alone, before any constraint extraction or solver invocation. When $C_0$ falls below 5\%, SICA degenerates to SC by construction. High $C_0$ is necessary but not sufficient, as the capability trap renders constraints non-discriminative even when disagreement is abundant ($C_0 = 61.8\%$, $\Delta = -2.45$~pp, n.s., for LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO). The recommended workflow is: compute $C_0$, and if it exceeds 5\%, assess constraint quality on a small pilot before committing to full-scale extraction.
13	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UXJiNL43nPN7kWGoLTA9g4`</summary>

```text
49	\end{table*}
50	
51	The four confirmatory conditions ($n \geq 200$) span two models (Qwen2.5-14B and LLaMA-3.1-8B) across formal logic, commonsense, and deductive reasoning (combined $N = 1{,}208$). Across these conditions, $\Delta$ ranges from $-2.45$ to $+2.00$~pp, with no condition reaching significance ($p = 0.267$ and $0.424$ for the two nonzero gaps). The seven exploratory conditions ($n \leq 60$) cover the remaining four models (Qwen2.5-7B, Mistral-7B, DeepSeek-R1-7B, QwQ-32B) and are consistent with this null pattern: $\Delta$ ranges from $-3.33$ to $+3.33$~pp, all nonsignificant. QwQ-32B on AIME produces identical outputs ($C_0 = 2.6\%$), confirming that reasoning-specialized models leave no room for SICA to operate.
52	
53	Across eleven conditions, six models, and more than 1,500 instances (four confirmatory conditions with $n \geq 200$, seven exploratory conditions with $n \leq 60$), SICA does not improve upon majority voting. The oracle rows tell a different story: gold constraints yield $+8.82$~pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$~pp on ProofWriter (both $p < 0.001$), proving the aggregation mechanism works when given discriminative constraints.
54	
55	
56	\subsection{Oracle Control: The Aggregation Mechanism Is Effective}
57	\label{sec:oracle}
58	
59	We construct oracle variants that replace self-extracted constraints with gold annotations: FOL formulas from FOLIO ($n = 204$) and DSL logic programs from ProofWriter ($n = 600$). The oracle uses Z3 to evaluate each gold constraint against each candidate answer directly, rather than the source-trace attribution used in self-extraction mode (\S\ref{sec:maxsat}). This design isolates the constraint quality variable: any improvement is attributable to gold constraints, not scoring mechanics. The constraint ablation (\S\ref{sec:ablation}) independently confirms that the scoring mechanism is irrelevant under self-extraction (803/804 agreement with count-only).
60	
61	On FOLIO, Oracle-SICA achieves 88.24\% accuracy versus 79.41\% for both SC and standard SICA ($+8.82$~pp, McNemar $p = 6.15 \times 10^{-5}$). Z3 checks on gold annotations agree with ground-truth labels for 193/204 problems (94.6\%). On ProofWriter, Oracle-SICA achieves 86.67\% versus 75.17\% for SC ($+11.50$~pp, McNemar $p = 9.21 \times 10^{-15}$), with Z3 gold agreement of 97.1\% (577/594). The ProofWriter gain is larger and more significant, with relational subtypes benefiting most: RelNeg $+17.86$~pp, RelNoneg $+13.01$~pp (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:pw_oracle_subtype}). Across both datasets ($N = 804$), oracle constraints consistently and significantly improve aggregation, establishing that the bottleneck lies primarily in extraction quality.
62	
63
```

```text
109	\end{table}
110	
111	MAX-SAT and count-only agree on 803 of 804 predictions, confirming that the satisfiability solver contributes nothing beyond vote counting (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}). Against SC, all three variants disagree on 30--40 predictions (773--774 for SICA and count-only, 764 for random-weight), but wins and losses cancel: among SICA's 31 discordant pairs with SC, 16 favor SICA, 14 favor SC, and 1 produces different wrong answers, yielding a net gain of only $+$2 problems. Random-weight deviates most from SC (764/804) and achieves the lowest accuracy (75.87\%), confirming that randomizing constraint weights introduces noise without discriminative benefit. Self-extracted constraints carry no signal beyond vote counts. The oracle control ($+8.82$~pp) confirms that the aggregation mechanism works given independent problem structure; self-extracted constraints simply fail to provide it.
112
```

```text
6. appendix.tex:275 - "confirming that the deterministic DSL-to-Z3 translation faithfully preserves problem semantics" - trailing -ing phrase.

7. appendix.tex:281 - "suggesting longer reasoning chains benefit more from oracle constraints" - trailing -ing phrase in caption.

I'm noticing a recurring pattern of trailing -ing constructions across multiple files, which is a common stylistic marker in academic writing. While each instance individually is acceptable, the frequency suggests this might be worth addressing if the goal is to reduce AI-like writing patterns.

Most of these follow the "confirming/establishing that..." structure, which is reasonably natural. I should also scan for other potential markers like overuse of transition words and phrases like "It is worth noting" to get a fuller picture of the writing style.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Interesting - the experiment_plan.yaml has 19 entries, but many have `status: "planned"` which means they don't have a registry_id mapping. This is because the experiment plan was created early and many experiments were run with different registry IDs than planned. Let me count what's actually done:

From the plan:
- exp-001 (sica_expanded_14b): DONE ✓
- exp-002 (K ablation): planned (but data exists from K ablation runs)


- exp-003 through exp-010 mostly show "planned" status, though several have actual completed work in the registry under different names - exp-004 has the 7b scaling results, exp-005 has QwQ results, exp-009 has the overhead analysis. A few like exp-007 (72B AWQ) were deprioritized and never completed, while others like exp-008 (case studies) and exp-010 (Mistral) are still pending.

Looking at the next batch, exp-010 through exp-019 follow a similar pattern where most are marked planned but have actual completed runs - exp-011 has the LLaMA 8b results, exp-012 has the GSM8K pilot, exp-013 has the ablation study, exp-014 has DeepSeek runs, and exp-017 through exp-019 all have completed versions in the registry. The gaps are exp-015 (Logic-LM baseline) and exp-016 (Weighted SC) which remain unfinished, and exp-019 appears to be cut off mid-entry.

The core issue is that the plan has stale registry mappings since experiments were actually run under different IDs than originally planned. The paper itself draws from all the completed experiments tracked in the registry, so this is really just a documentation mismatch rather than missing work.

Checking against the paper's anticipated reviewer concerns, I can see the critical experiments are covered: statistical significance is addressed through four powered tests, the "just a tiebreaker" concern is handled by the K ablation and constraint ablation studies, model family diversity is confirmed across Qwen, Mistral, LLaMA, and DeepSeek, and there's coverage across multiple reasoning datasets.

Two experiments didn't get done—the 72B-AWQ scaling test and the Logic-LM baseline—but the paper already acknowledges the scaling question as an open limitation, and the Logic-LM comparison is addressed in the Related Work section.

The oracle experiments weren't in the original plan but emerged as the paper's strongest contribution. Looking at this from a reviewer's perspective, the main vulnerabilities are straightforward: only two datasets for oracle validation, zero-shot extraction prompts, and missing comparisons with some baselines—but all of these are already flagged in the limitations.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The Director is asking me to evaluate the novelty defense strategy against the reviewer concern that the "capability trap" is structurally isomorphic to Stechly et al. (2025)'s self-verification findings. This is a critical strategic question for the paper's acceptance at EMNLP 2026.

Let me think carefully about this from a strict EMNLP reviewer perspective.

First, let me understand the concern: Stechly et al. (2025) showed that LLM self-verification on reasoning/planning tasks degrades performance, while sound external verification yields significant gains. Their finding that "verification is not inherently easier than generation" is indeed structurally parallel to this paper's "capability trap" - same model can't extract useful constraints from its own reasoning.


Now I'm working through the defense layers. The first argument about FOL+SAT having higher success priors due to Z3 soundness is actually quite weak—a reviewer would point out that the paper's own results contradict this hypothesis, making it a null result rather than a novelty claim. The second layer about empirical contributions being independent needs careful examination around the dual-domain oracle and dual-metric aspects.

The oracle control methodology is genuinely novel—no prior work has isolated extraction versus aggregation failures through component replacement, quantified constraint discriminativeness at the per-constraint level, or proposed C0 as a zero-cost diagnostic. The mechanism analysis revealing why self-extracted constraints fail semantically is moderately strong, showing more granularity than prior observations. The related work positioning acknowledging the isomorphism while distinguishing formal versus natural language approaches is necessary but insufficient on its own.

A skeptical reviewer would likely argue this just confirms Stechly et al.'s finding that self-verification fails, now in a FOL+SAT setting—the oracle experiment being unsurprising since gold annotations naturally outperform self-extracted ones. But the real contribution isn't that self-verification doesn't work; it's the quantitative mechanism diagnosis showing *why* it fails at a specific granularity, identifying constraint non-discriminativeness rather than incorrectness as the failure mode, and using causal isolation through the oracle to separate these effects.

The novelty hinges on moving from "X doesn't work" to "here's exactly why X doesn't work with causal evidence, plus a practical diagnostic tool and cost quantification"—that distinction from Stechly is what matters for a top venue. A 70B experiment would help with scaling but wouldn't directly address the novelty gap, though it would show the problem persists at larger model sizes. For EMNLP 2026, the question becomes whether this mechanistic depth and methodological rigor push the overall assessment high enough for acceptance.

Looking at the likely reviewer scores: the methodology is solid (3.5-4), but the core finding—that self-verification fails in this new setting—is somewhat confirmatory of existing work (2.5-3 on significance and novelty). That trajectory points toward Findings territory (Overall ~3-3.5) rather than Main conference. To break into the Main tier, I'd need to either deepen the "why" story beyond what Stechly provides, add a positive result showing how to escape the trap, or lean harder into the practical diagnostic contribution.

Now I'm thinking through how to reposition this work. The key is reframing it not as "we tested self-extraction and it failed" but as "we've pinpointed the causal mechanism—semantic coupling, not extraction errors—with quantitative evidence and a deployable diagnostic." That's a stronger narrative. And the mechanistic insight is actually more granular than Stechly's claim: rather than just "verification ≈ generation in difficulty," I can show that self-extracted constraints are syntactically valid but semantically non-discriminative because they encode shared domain facts instead of answer-specific logic. The oracle methodology is my strongest asset here—no prior work has done this kind of component-level analysis—so I should emphasize that angle and broaden the validation to make it more compelling. The paper also functions as a reusable methodology template for evaluating neuro-symbolic self-extraction pipelines, and it provides practical value by steering future researchers away from this path—a negative result that prevents wasted effort. Beyond that, the dual-metric gap between source-trace correlation and Z3 semantic discriminativeness reveals something about constraint structure that Stechly et al. didn't capture: statistical correlation doesn't guarantee semantic discriminativeness.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RioT65TmRXpZEQ9feJWWue`</summary>

```text
19	\paragraph{Debate and Multi-Agent Aggregation.}
20	Multi-agent debate refines answers through iterative natural language discussion among LLM instances \citep{liang2023encouraging,du2023improving}. In debate, models exchange arguments in plain text and update their positions based on peer responses. SICA takes a structurally different approach: instead of natural language refinement, it extracts formal constraints and resolves disagreements through satisfiability optimization. Both families attempt to move beyond simple voting, but debate preserves natural language flexibility while SICA commits to formal structure. \citet{huang2024selfcorrect} show that LLMs struggle to self-correct reasoning without external feedback, and \citet{mirzadeh2024gsmsymbolic} demonstrate that LLM mathematical reasoning is fragile under symbolic perturbations. \citet{stechly2025selfverification} provide the most direct parallel: they show that LLM self-verification on reasoning and planning tasks degrades performance, while sound external verification yields significant gains. Their finding that verification is not inherently easier than generation for these models is structurally isomorphic to our capability trap, but operates in natural language self-critique rather than FOL+SAT. Our contribution extends this line by demonstrating the same limitation in a formal symbolic setting: even when self-extracted constraints are syntactically valid FOL and processed by a sound solver, they carry no discriminative signal beyond vote counting. \citet{valmeekam2023planning} and \citet{kambhampati2024position} further establish that LLMs lack autonomous planning ability and require external modules in ``LLM-Modulo'' frameworks to achieve reliable performance, reinforcing the pattern that self-reliance is the bottleneck. Across all six lines of work reviewed here, the consistent pattern is that improving on majority voting requires information beyond what the reasoning model can extract from its own outputs, whether from trained verifiers, external solvers, human annotations, or cross-model interaction.
```

```json
      "content": "\\paragraph{Debate and Multi-Agent Aggregation.}\nMulti-agent debate refines answers through iterative natural language discussion among LLM instances \\citep{liang2023encouraging,du2023improving}. In debate, models exchange arguments in plain text and update their positions based on peer responses. SICA takes a structurally different approach: instead of natural language refinement, it extracts formal constraints and resolves disagreements through satisfiability optimization. Both families attempt to move beyond simple voting, but debate preserves natural language flexibility while SICA commits to formal structure. \\citet{huang2024selfcorrect} show that LLMs struggle to self-correct reasoning without external feedback, and \\citet{mirzadeh2024gsmsymbolic} demonstrate that LLM mathematical reasoning is fragile under symbolic perturbations. \\citet{stechly2025selfverification} provide the most direct parallel: they show that LLM self-verification on reasoning and planning tasks degrades performance, while sound external verification yields significant gains. Their finding that verification is not inherently easier than generation for these models is structurally isomorphic to our capability trap, but operates in natural language self-critique rather than FOL+SAT. Our contribution extends this line by demonstrating the same limitation in a formal symbolic setting: even when self-extracted constraints are syntactically valid FOL and processed by a sound solver, they carry no discriminative signal beyond vote counting. \\citet{valmeekam2023planning} and \\citet{kambhampati2024position} further establish that LLMs lack autonomous planning ability and require external modules in ``LLM-Modulo'' frameworks to achieve reliable performance, reinforcing the pattern that self-reliance is the bottleneck. Across all six lines of work reviewed here, the consistent pattern is that improving on majority voting requires information beyond what the reasoning model can extract from its own outputs, whether from trained verifiers, external solvers, human annotations, or cross-model interaction.",
      "numLines": 2,
      "startLine": 19,
      "totalLines": 20
    }
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CriZA3DJX5AEduJq7a2oZy`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Neuro-symbolic methods that augment large language model (LLM) reasoning with formal solvers have demonstrated gains on logical reasoning benchmarks. Logic-LM \citep{pan2023logiclm} and LINC \citep{olausson2023linc} delegate formalization to external provers. More recent systems route problems to specialized solver paradigms \citep{xu2026dylos}, apply logic-complete decomposition \citep{li2025aristotle}, train dedicated first-order logic (FOL) verifiers on supervised data \citep{wang2025fover}, or employ multi-perspective grounding \citep{luo2025logicagent}. These systems report positive results on formal reasoning benchmarks, but a critical commonality unites them: each introduces capabilities beyond the reasoning model itself, whether trained verifiers, multi-paradigm solver selection, or external knowledge bases. The external machinery is effective but imposes practical costs in training data, integration complexity, and domain-specific engineering that limits portability.
4	
5	These results raise a direct question: can the reasoning model itself extract useful formal constraints from its own traces? Same-model self-extraction would require no training data, no external prover, and no second model. There is reason to expect this approach could succeed where natural language self-critique fails \citep{huang2024selfcorrect}: FOL eliminates the ambiguity of natural language verification, and the downstream solver (Z3) provides formally guaranteed soundness, so any correctly extracted constraint contributes provably valid reasoning. Practitioners could augment any LLM with symbolic verification by adding a single extraction prompt and a satisfiability solver, with no domain-specific infrastructure. If successful, this approach would provide a lightweight, portable alternative to the external machinery that current systems require. Yet no prior work has systematically evaluated whether self-extraction produces constraints useful for answer aggregation, or characterized the conditions under which it succeeds or fails. This gap is consequential: the practical promise of neuro-symbolic reasoning hinges on whether the reasoning model alone can supply formal structure of sufficient quality, or whether external grounding is fundamentally required.
6	
7	Self-Consistency (SC) selects the most frequent answer across multiple chain-of-thought samples \citep{wang2023sc} and remains competitive across mathematical, logical, and commonsense reasoning \citep{chen2024usc, taubenfeld2025cisc}. SC treats traces as black boxes, discarding all intermediate reasoning. Each trace, however, contains intermediate steps, logical dependencies, and structural relationships that could in principle ground aggregation in formal logic, adding discriminative power beyond vote counting. This would also address SC's known scaling plateau, where additional traces yield diminishing returns \citep{loo2025reevaluating}.
8	
9	We evaluate this possibility through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), a pipeline that prompts a single LLM to extract FOL-style constraints (in practice predominantly ground propositional clauses; \S\ref{sec:constraint-extraction}) from $K$ reasoning traces, deduplicates by syntactic equivalence, and solves a weighted partial MAX-SAT problem via Z3 \citep{demoura2008z3}. The answer best supported by the largest satisfiable constraint subset is selected (Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}). We test six models spanning three base architectures (Qwen, Mistral, LLaMA) from 7B to 32B parameters (Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, QwQ-32B), including instruction-tuned models, a reasoning-specialized model, and a distilled reasoning model, across three reasoning domains: formal logic (FOLIO \citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}), commonsense (StrategyQA \citep{geva2021strategyqa}), and science (ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}), with supplementary probes on GSM8K \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k} and AIME 2024, totaling more than 1,500 instances. Oracle controls on FOLIO ($n=204$) and ProofWriter ($n=600$) replace self-extracted constraints with gold annotations verified by Z3, isolating extraction quality from aggregation effectiveness following established methodology \citep{press2023compositionality}.
10	
11	Across all conditions, SICA provides no statistically significant improvement over SC, with accuracy gaps between $-3.3$ and $+3.3$\,pp. The oracle experiments yield a fundamentally different result: $+8.82$\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\,pp on ProofWriter (both $p < 0.001$). The MAX-SAT mechanism is sound when given discriminative constraints; the bottleneck is extraction quality. Because the same model generates both answers and constraints, the extracted constraints are semantically coupled to their source answers and carry no independent signal. We term this the \emph{capability trap}: the model cannot formalize corrections to its own reasoning errors because extraction inherits the same biases that produced those errors. A constraint ablation confirms the diagnosis: MAX-SAT and count-only aggregation agree on 803 of 804 predictions, and SICA's 31 disagreements with SC approximately cancel (16 wins, 14 losses, 1 both-wrong with different answers), yielding a net gain of only $+$2 problems (\S\ref{sec:ablation}). Full-dataset analysis shows only 8.2\% of unique constraints are semantically discriminative across candidates (95\% CI [7.6, 8.8]; $N = 804$); the remainder encode shared domain facts that contribute identical scores to every answer. Constraint extraction accounts for 48.7\% of total SICA pipeline time, roughly doubling wall-clock cost for zero expected accuracy gain.
12	
13	Our contributions are:
14	\begin{itemize}
15	\item We characterize the capability trap in same-model constraint extraction: across six models spanning three base architectures and three reasoning domains ($N > 1{,}500$), self-extracted SICA provides no statistically significant improvement over majority voting, and only 8.2\% of constraints are semantically discriminative across candidates (\S\ref{sec:diagnostics}, \S\ref{sec:ablation}).
16	\item We causally localize the failure via oracle controls on two datasets: replacing self-extraction with gold annotations yields $+8.82$\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\,pp on ProofWriter (both $p < 0.001$), proving the aggregation mechanism is sound and the bottleneck lies primarily in extraction quality (\S\ref{sec:oracle}).
17	\item We quantify the recoverable gap: the $+8.82$/$+11.50$\,pp oracle ceilings and the 48.7\% cost overhead of non-discriminative extraction jointly establish the barrier and the potential for future methods targeting the extraction bottleneck (\S\ref{sec:ablation}, \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
18	\item We propose the multi-candidate rate $C_0$ as a zero-cost, necessary-condition diagnostic for practitioners evaluating symbolic aggregation (\S\ref{sec:c0}).
19	\end{itemize}
```

```json
  "text": "[severity: warning] 新颖性防御策略深度评估——对 R3-N4 (Stechly 同构) 的回应\n\n## 一、四层防御强弱排序\n\n**最强 → 最弱: Layer 2 > Layer 3 > Layer 4 > Layer 1**\n\n### Layer 2（实证方法论）— 最强牌\nOracle 因果隔离是本文独有的方法论贡献。Stechly 只证明 \"self-verification hurts\"（二元结论），本文通过组件替换实验精确定位失败源：extraction（失败）vs aggregation（有效）。这种 component-level causal diagnosis 在 neuro-symbolic pipeline 评估中没有先例。双域 oracle（FOLIO +8.82pp, PW +11.50pp）不仅证明 \"self-extraction 不行\"，还量化了 \"gold extraction 能好多少\"——Stechly 没有这种 recoverable gap 量化。\n\n**建议加强写法**：Introduction 第二条贡献应更突出方法论新颖性。当前写的是 \"We causally localize the failure via oracle controls\"，建议改为强调这是一个 **可复用的评估框架**，不仅适用于 SICA，适用于任何 neuro-symbolic self-extraction pipeline。\n\n### Layer 3（机制分析）— 中等偏强\n39.2% source-trace vs 8.2% Z3 semantic 的双指标差距揭示了 Stechly 完全没有的机制细节：constraints 是 **统计关联但语义等价的**（statistically correlated with source answers but semantically equivalent across all candidates）。这不是 \"验证和生成一样难\" 的重复，而是对 WHY 的精细解剖。803/804 agreement 更是把 \"MAX-SAT 退化为计数\" 这一结论钉死。\n\n**当前问题**：这个 dual-metric 分析在 Discussion 中才展开（§5 第2段），但它是区分本文与 Stechly 的核心论据——应该在 Introduction 的贡献列表中更显眼。当前第一条贡献只提了 8.2%，没提 39.2% 和两者差距的含义。\n\n**建议加强**：在贡献第一条或第三条中，加一句话明确说 \"the gap between source-trace discriminativeness (39.2%) and Z3 semantic discriminativeness (8.2%) reveals that constraints are statistically coupled to source answers but semantically vacuous across candidates — a structural diagnosis absent from prior work on self-verification\"。\n\n### Layer 4（Related Work 定位）— 必要但不足\n当前 related_work.tex L20 的定位是准确的：承认同构，指出 formal vs NL 区别，声明 \"extends this line\"。但 \"extends\" 这个词偏弱——审稿人会读成 \"incremental extension\"。\n\n**建议修改**：将 \"Our contribution extends this line by demonstrating the same limitation in a formal symbolic setting\" 改为更精确的差异化表述，强调本文提供的不是 **相同发现在新 setting** 而是 **不同类型的证据**。例如：\"Where Stechly et al. establish a binary outcome (self-verification hurts), we provide causal decomposition (oracle isolation), quantitative mechanism diagnosis (dual discriminativeness metrics), and a deployable screening criterion (C0) — a different category of contribution that explains *why* the limitation holds and *how much* is recoverable.\"\n\n### Layer 1（先验预期）— 最弱，建议弱化\n\"FOL+Z3 soundness 给了更高的成功先验\" 这个论证方向有风险：审稿人会反驳 \"you expected it to work, it didn't, that's a null result not a novelty.\" Introduction 第2段当前的措辞 (\"There is reason to expect this approach could succeed...\") 设置了一个期望，然后结果打脸——这本身没问题（negative result papers 都这样），但不应把 \"先验预期高\" 当作新颖性防御。\n\n**建议**：保留 motivation 中的先验论证（解释为什么值得测试），但不要在 rebuttal 中把它当作新颖性论据。\n\n## 二、我们可能忽略的防御角度\n\n### 角度 A：方法论模板的可迁移性\n本文的 oracle + discriminative rate + C0 评估框架不仅适用于 SICA，可用于评估 **任何** neuro-symbolic self-extraction pipeline（包括 Logic-LM 的 self-refinement、LINC 的 FOL translation 等）。这是一个 reusable evaluation methodology 贡献。Stechly 没有提供这种可迁移的诊断工具。\n\n**建议**：在 Discussion 末尾或 Contribution 列表中明确提一句：the evaluation framework (oracle isolation + dual discriminativeness metrics + C0) is applicable to any pipeline where an LLM extracts formal structure from its own outputs.\n\n### 角度 B：统计关联 ≠ 语义判别力（这是本文独有的 insight）\n39.2% vs 8.2% 这个发现的含义是深刻的：**constraints 看起来与 source answer 相关（因为来自同一个 trace），但这种关联是 spurious 的，因为 Z3 判定它们对所有 candidate 等价**。这个 \"statistical correlation without semantic discriminativeness\" 的结构性发现是 Stechly 完全没有的——Stechly 没有任何机制分析。这应该被更突出地 frame。\n\n### 角度 C：Negative result 的节约价值\n本文 48.7% overhead 分析+零收益结论 = 帮助 practitioners 避免投入资源在 self-extraction 路径上。这个 practical value 在 EMNLP（应用导向社区）有额外分量。\n\n## 三、70B 实验对新颖性的帮助\n\n**中等帮助，不解决核心问题。** \n\n70B 实验证明 capability trap 跨规模持续，增强 claim 的 scope（当前限制是 \"7B-32B only\"）。但审稿人的新颖性质疑不是 \"你的 scale 不够\"，而是 \"Stechly 已经说过了\"。70B 实验不改变与 Stechly 的区分度。\n\n**但值得做的理由**：它消除 limitations 中最显眼的弱点（\"70B+ untested\"），堵住一个独立的 reviewer 攻击面。如果 GPU 预算允许且不延误 DDL，建议做。优先级低于论文文本修改。\n\n## 四、Track 建议\n\n**不在我的职责范围内做 venue 决策**（按 CLAUDE.md 规定）。但可以提供审稿人视角的分析：\n\n从 EMNLP ARR 评分标准看：\n- Soundness: 3.5-4（方法论严谨，oracle 设计优秀）\n- Significance: 2.5-3.5（取决于审稿人是否认为机制分析构成独立贡献）\n- Novelty: 2.5-3.5（关键分歧点——加强上述防御可推向高端）\n- Clarity: 3.5-4（写作质量好）\n\n**新颖性是能否从 Findings（Overall 3）推到 Main（Overall 4）的关键变量。** 上述文本修改不需要新实验，但可能将 Novelty 从 2.5 推到 3-3.5。\n\n## 五、具体修改建议摘要\n\n| 位置 | 当前问题 | 修改方向 |\n|------|---------|---------|\n| Introduction 贡献列表 | 机制分析新颖性不显眼 | 第1条或第3条加入 dual-metric gap 的含义 |\n| Related Work L20 | \"extends this line\" 读成 incremental | 改为强调 different category of evidence |\n| Discussion 末尾 | 评估框架的可迁移性未提 | 加一句 reusable methodology 声明 |\n| Introduction §2 | 先验论证设置了 \"期望高→失败\" 叙事 | 保留 motivation 但明确 framing：值得测试是因为 gap in literature（无人系统评估过），不仅因为 FOL soundness |\n\n以上修改均为文本层面，不需要新实验，可在 1-2 小时内完成。",
  "project": "symb_invariant_consensus"
}
```
